For convenience, we also include a similar simulation here. Let  $\pmb{v} = (0, \mathbf{1}_s, \mathbf{0}_{p - s - 1}) / \sqrt{s}$ . That is,  $\pmb{v}$  has unit  $\ell_2$ -norm, sparsity  $s$ , and is constant on its active set. We take  $s = 100$ ,  $n = 500$ ,  $p = 1000$ ,  $\rho^2 = 0.75$ ,  $\sigma = 1$ ,  $\lambda = \sqrt{2\sigma \log(p / s)}$ ,  $\pmb{\theta}^{*} = 3\sqrt{s}\lambda \pmb{v}$ , and  $\pmb{\Sigma} = \mathbf{I}_p + \rho \pmb{e}_1 \pmb{v}^\top + \rho \pmb{v} \pmb{e}_1^\top$ . One can check that  $\pmb{\Sigma}$  is positive definite. For 5000 replications, we generate data from the model (2), fit the debiased Lasso estimate  $\widehat{\theta}_1^{\mathrm{d}}$ , compute the estimated standard error  $\widehat{\tau}$ , and compute

$$
\mathrm {s d} _ {\mathrm {B Z}} ^ {2} := \frac {\| \boldsymbol {y} - \boldsymbol {X} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \| _ {2} ^ {2}}{n \left(1 - \| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \| _ {0} / n\right) ^ {2}} + \frac {\left(\widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {j} - \theta_ {j} ^ {*}\right) ^ {2}}{1 - \| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \| _ {0} / n}. \tag {24}
$$

In Figure 2, from left to right, we plot histograms of  $\sqrt{1 - \rho^2}\sqrt{n} (\widehat{\theta_1^{\mathrm{d}}} -\theta_1^*) / \widehat{\tau}$ ,  $\sqrt{n} (\widehat{\theta_1^{\mathrm{d}}} -\theta_1^*) / \mathrm{sd}_{\mathrm{BZ}}$ , and  $\hat{\theta}_1 - \theta_1^*$ . In the first two plots, we superimpose the standard normal density. In the left plot, we see an overdispersion of  $\sqrt{1 - \rho^2}\sqrt{n} (\widehat{\theta_1^{\mathrm{d}}} -\theta_1^*) / \widehat{\tau}$  relative to the normal density which is no longer present when the errors are instead normalized by  $\mathrm{sd}_{\mathrm{BZ}}$  in the second plot. This validates that for the first coordinate,  $\sqrt{1 - \rho^2}\widehat{\tau} /\sqrt{n}$  underestimates the standard error. The right-most histogram show that the error  $\hat{\theta}_1 - \theta_1^*$  is of order 1, whence the second term in  $\mathrm{sd}_{\mathrm{BZ}}$  is non-negligible. (Precisely, the standard deviation in this plot is about 2.2). We emphasize that  $\mathrm{sd}_{\mathrm{BZ}}$  is not an empirical quantity. Our purpose is simply to display evidence that the standard error  $\Sigma_{1| - 1}^{-1 / 2}\widehat{\tau}$  is incorrect for the first coordinate. The paper [13] also provides an empirical standard error which agrees with  $\mathrm{sd}_{\mathrm{BZ}}$  to first order.

Figure 2 suggests the conjecture that while  $\widehat{\theta}_{1}^{\mathrm{d}}$  may have standard error larger than  $\tau^{*}$  in some coordinates, it is still approximately normally distributed and unbiased. We do not establish this fact, and as far as we know, establishing it remains open. We expect that completing this theory will require different techniques than those in the current work.

An alternative approach. In the current paper, we instead provide an alternative construction of confidence intervals for a single coordinate using a leave-one-out technique. We are able to establish the coordinate-wise validity of these confidence intervals even in cases where the Lasso error  $\widehat{\theta_j} -\theta_j^*$  is of order 1. We call these confidence intervals, defined below, the leave-one-out confidence intervals, denoted by  $\mathsf{Cl}_j^{\mathrm{loo}}$ . According to simulation, the leave-one-out confidence intervals often approximately agree with the debiased confidence intervals, though for some coordinates they may have a larger or smaller width.

![](images/69c4faa259263f2ee67607c0a57208dcc4c3cdfcc58d6168f42562d62c722449.jpg)  
Fig 2. The debiased Lasso test statistic  $\widehat{\theta}_{1}^{\mathrm{d}}$  for  $p = 1000$ ,  $n = 500$ ,  $s = 100$ ,  $\rho^{2} = .5$ ,  $\sigma = 1$ ,  $\lambda = \sqrt{2\sigma\log(p / s) / n} = .096$ ,  $\boldsymbol{\theta}^{*} = 3\sqrt{s}\lambda \boldsymbol{v}$ , where  $\boldsymbol{v} = (0, \mathbf{1}_s, \mathbf{0}_{p - s - 1}) / \sqrt{s}$  and  $\boldsymbol{\Sigma} = \mathbf{I}_p + \rho e_1 \boldsymbol{v}^\top + \rho \boldsymbol{v} e_1^\top$ . On the left, we plot a histogram of the debiased Lasso centered and normalized based on the effective nosie  $\widehat{\tau}$  and the theory in this paper, and we superimpose the standard normal density. In the center plot, we normalize instead by the standard deviation derived in [12] (see Eq. (24)). On the right, we plot a histograms of Lasso error  $\widehat{\theta}_{1} - \theta_{1}^{*}$  without centering or standardization, demonstrating that the error of the Lasso in this coordinate is  $O(1)$ .

![](images/33d8621a56c7472cb2a471d2d980226ed73bb9cf1252b79bcf5ba9972fd4767e.jpg)

![](images/87ac762abf423af47247f6e7e643877faeea9ad9081e6695849cf0f3a135e637.jpg)

To facilitate the construction, let us write the observation vector  $\pmb{y}$  as

$$
\boldsymbol {y} = \left(\dots \check {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {j} \dots\right) \left( \begin{array}{c} \vdots \\ \theta_ {j} ^ {*} \\ \vdots \end{array} \right) + \sigma z = \theta_ {j} ^ {*} \check {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {j} + \boldsymbol {X} _ {- j} \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {- j} ^ {*} + \sigma z, \tag {25}
$$

where  $X_{-j} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times (p - 1)}$  denotes the original design matrix excluding the  $j$ -th column and  $\check{x}_j$  denotes the  $j$ -th column. Define  $\check{x}_j^{\perp} := \check{x}_j - X_{-j}\Sigma_{-j, -j}^{-1}\Sigma_{-j,j} \in \mathbb{R}^n$  so that  $\check{x}_j^{\perp}$  is independent of  $X_{-j}$  (see Section C.3). Let  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}}$  be any deterministic real number that is chosen a priori; for instance,  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}}$  can be set as 0. According to decomposition (25),

$$
\boldsymbol {y} - \check {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {j} ^ {\perp} \widehat {\theta} _ {j, \text {i n i t}} = \boldsymbol {X} _ {- j} \underbrace {\left(\boldsymbol {\theta} _ {- j} ^ {*} + \left(\theta_ {j} ^ {*} - \widehat {\theta} _ {j , \text {i n i t}}\right) \boldsymbol {\Sigma} _ {- j , - j} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\Sigma} _ {- j , j}\right)} _ {=: \boldsymbol {\theta} _ {\mathrm {l o o}} ^ {*}} + \check {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {j} ^ {\perp} \left(\theta_ {j} ^ {*} - \widehat {\theta} _ {j, \text {i n i t}}\right) + \sigma \boldsymbol {z}, \tag {26}
$$

and

$$
\check {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {j} ^ {\perp} (\theta_ {j} ^ {*} - \widehat {\theta} _ {j, \mathrm {i n i t}}) + \sigma \boldsymbol {z} \sim \mathsf {N} (0, \sigma_ {\mathrm {l o o}} ^ {2} \mathbf {I} _ {n}) \mathrm {w i t h} \sigma_ {\mathrm {l o o}} ^ {2} := \sigma^ {2} + \Sigma_ {j | - j} (\theta_ {j} ^ {*} - \widehat {\theta} _ {j, \mathrm {i n i t}}) ^ {2}.
$$

Expression (26) can be viewed as defining a linear-model with  $p - 1$  covariates, with true parameter  $\theta_{\mathrm{loo}}^*$ , noise variance  $\sigma_{\mathrm{loo}}^2$ , and outcome  $\pmb{y} - \check{x}_j^\perp \widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}}$ . We call this the leave-one-out model, and call

$$
\boldsymbol {y} _ {\text {i n i t}} := \boldsymbol {y} - \check {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {j} ^ {\perp} \widehat {\theta} _ {j, \text {i n i t}}
$$

the pseudo-outcome. Let  $\tau_{\mathrm{loo}}^{*}$ ,  $\zeta_{\mathrm{loo}}^{*}$  be the solution to the fixed point equations (9a) and (9b) in the leave-one-out model, and  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}_{\mathrm{loo}}$  be the Lasso fit on  $\pmb{y}_{\mathrm{init}}$  to  $X_{-j}$ .

The leave-one-out confidence interval is then constructed based on the variable importance statistic

$$
\xi_ {j} := \widehat {\theta} _ {j, \text {i n i t}} + \frac {\left(\check {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {j} ^ {\perp}\right) ^ {\top} \left(\boldsymbol {y} _ {\text {i n i t}} - \boldsymbol {X} _ {- j} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\text {l o o}}\right)}{\Sigma_ {j | - j} (n - \| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\text {l o o}} \| _ {0})}. \tag {27}
$$

Note the statistic  $\xi_{j}$  is a renormalized empirical correlation between residuals from two regressions: the population regression of feature  $j$  on the other features (i.e.,  $\check{x}_j^\perp$ ), and a

sample regression of the pseudo-outcome  $\mathbf{y}_{\mathrm{init}}$  on the other features (i.e.,  $\mathbf{y}_{\mathrm{init}} - \mathbf{X}_{-j}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathrm{loo}}$ ). If  $\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j,\mathrm{init}} = \boldsymbol{\theta}_j^*$ , these residuals will be independent. Indeed, in this case  $\check{x}_j^\perp$  is independent of  $(\mathbf{y}_{\mathrm{init}},\mathbf{X}_{-j})$ , and because  $\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathrm{loo}}$  is a function of  $(\mathbf{y}_{\mathrm{init}},\mathbf{X}_{-j})$ ,  $\check{x}_j^\perp$  is also independent of  $\mathbf{y}_{\mathrm{init}} - \mathbf{X}_{-j}\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{\mathrm{loo}}$ . In this case, the distribution of  $\xi_j$  is easy to understand. We will also quantify the distribution of the variable importance statistic  $\xi_j$  when  $\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}_{j,\mathrm{init}}$  is sufficiently close to  $\boldsymbol{\theta}_j^*$ , which will allow us to construct tests and confidence intervals.

Similarly to  $\widehat{\tau} (\lambda)$  defined in Eq. (21), we estimate the effective noise level in the leave-one-out model by

$$
\widehat {\tau} _ {\mathrm {l o o}} ^ {j} := \frac {\| \pmb {y} _ {\mathrm {i n i t}} - \pmb {X} _ {- j} \widehat {\pmb {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {l o o}} \| _ {2}}{\sqrt {n} (1 - \| \widehat {\pmb {\theta}} _ {\mathrm {l o o}} \| _ {0} / n)}.
$$

The leave-one-out confidence interval is then defined as

$$
\mathsf {C l} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {l o o}} := \left[ \xi_ {j} - \Sigma_ {j | - j} ^ {- 1 / 2} \widehat {\tau} _ {\mathrm {l o o}} ^ {j} z _ {1 - \alpha / 2} / \sqrt {n}, \xi_ {j} + \Sigma_ {j | - j} ^ {- 1 / 2} \widehat {\tau} _ {\mathrm {l o o}} ^ {j} z _ {1 - \alpha / 2} / \sqrt {n} \right]. \tag {28}
$$

As asserted by the following result, this confidence interval  $\mathsf{Cl}_j^{\mathrm{loo}}$  achieves approximate coverage for fixed  $j$  provided  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}} - \theta_j^* = o(1)$ . We prove this result in Section B.8.2.

THEOREM 12. Assume  $p \geq 2$  and that the leave-one-out model and Lasso estimators satisfy (A1). Recall  $\tau_{\mathrm{loo}}^*$ ,  $\zeta_{\mathrm{loo}}^*$  are the solution to the fixed point equations (9a) and (9b) in the leave-one-out model.

(a) (Coverage and power of the leave-one-out confidence interval) For any  $\gamma > 0$ , there exist constants  $C, c, c' > 0$  depending only on  $\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}$  and  $\gamma$  such that for all  $\epsilon < c'$ ,  $|\theta_j^* - \widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}}| < c'$ , and  $\theta \in \mathbb{R}$ , we have

$$
\begin{array}{l} \left| \mathbb {P} \left(\theta \not \in \mathsf {C l} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {l o o}}\right) - \mathbb {P} \left(| \theta_ {j} ^ {*} + \Sigma_ {j | - j} ^ {- 1 / 2} \tau_ {\mathrm {l o o}} ^ {*} G / \sqrt {n} - \theta | > \Sigma_ {j | - j} ^ {- 1 / 2} \tau_ {\mathrm {l o o}} ^ {*} z _ {1 - \alpha / 2} / \sqrt {n}\right) \right| \\ \leq C \left(| \theta_ {j} ^ {*} - \widehat {\theta} _ {j, \mathrm {i n i t}} | ^ {2 / 3} + n ^ {2 / 6 + \gamma} | \theta_ {j} ^ {*} - \theta | + \frac {1}{n}\right), \\ \end{array}
$$

where  $G \sim \mathsf{N}(0,1)$ . (See discussion following theorem for an interpretation of this bound). (b) (Length of the leave-one-out confidence interval). There exist constants  $C, c, c' > 0$  depending only on  $\mathcal{P}_{\mathrm{model}}$ ,  $M'$ , and  $\delta_{\mathrm{loo}}$  such that for all  $\epsilon < c'$ ,

$$
\mathbb {P} _ {\theta_ {j} ^ {*}} \left(\left| \frac {\widehat {\tau} _ {\mathrm {l o o}} ^ {j}}{\tau_ {\mathrm {l o o}} ^ {*}} - 1 \right| > \epsilon\right) \leq \frac {C}{\epsilon^ {3}} e ^ {- c n \epsilon^ {6}}. \tag {29}
$$

Note that  $\mathbb{P}\left(|\theta_j^* + \Sigma_{j| - j}^{-1/2} \tau_{\mathrm{loo}}^* G / \sqrt{n} - \theta| > \tau_{\mathrm{loo}}^* z_{1 - \alpha/2} \Sigma_{j| - j}^{-1/2} / \sqrt{n}\right)$  is the power of the standard two-sided confidence interval under Gaussian observations  $\Sigma_{j| - j}^{1/2} \theta_j^* + \tau_{\mathrm{loo}}^* G / \sqrt{n}$  against alternative  $\theta$ . This normal approximation holds provided  $\theta_j^* - \hat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}} = o(1)$  and  $\theta - \theta_j^* = o(n^{-2/6 - \gamma})$  for some  $\gamma > 0$ . In particular, it holds for  $\theta - \theta_j^*$  on the  $n^{-1/2}$  scale.

It is convenient to consider a few special cases of Theorem 12:

1.  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}} = 0$  and  $\theta_{j}^{*} = 0$ . In this case, setting  $\theta = 0$  yields  $|\mathbb{P}\left(0\notin \mathsf{Cl}_j^{\mathrm{loc}}\right) - \alpha |\leq C / n$ . In fact a moment of reflection shows that this bound can be improved to yield

$$
\mathbb {P} \left(0 \notin \mathrm {C l} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {l o o}}\right) = \alpha .
$$

That is, we have exact control of type I errors.

2.  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}} = 0$  and  $\theta_{j}^{*} = o(1)$ . Setting again  $\theta = \theta_{j}^{*}$ , we obtain

$$
| \mathbb {P} \left(\theta_ {j} ^ {*} \notin \mathrm {C l} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {l o o}}\right) - \alpha | = o (1).
$$

That is, we obtain asymptotic coverage for all non-zero coefficients that are small (note that if  $\| \pmb{\theta}^{*}\|_{2} = O(1)$ , this is the case for most non-zero coefficients).

3. Generally leave-one-out confidence intervals are successful provided  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}}$  is consistent for  $\theta_{j}^{*}$ . Note that we assume  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}}$  is deterministic, which accommodates settings in which it is based on prior knowledge or is an estimate based on an independent data set. Note that consistency is a rather weak requirement (indeed  $\| \widehat{\pmb{\theta}} -\pmb{\theta}^{*}\|_{2} = O(1)$ ). We also point at the next section for a construction of exact confidence intervals that do not require the initialization  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}}$ .

REMARK 4.7. Even when  $\theta_{j}^{*}$  is 0, it is possible that  $\widehat{\theta}_{j}$  as estimated by the Lasso is of order 1; indeed, Figure 2 presents a simulation of such a scenario. In this case, the naïve standard error for the debiased Lasso is too small, but our leave-one-out construction with  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}} = 0$  achieves coverage. Moreover, in Section 5.2, we provide simulation evidence that in this scenario, the leave-one-out estimates  $\xi_{j}$  have smaller variance than the debiased estimates  $\widehat{\theta}_{j}^{\mathrm{d}}$ , indicating that they permit more precise inference. Characterizing in which scenarios the leave-one-out intervals are more or less precise than the debaised confidence intervals is a promising avenue for future work.

In concurrent work, Bellec and Zhang [12] consider debiasing with a arbitrary convex penalties, and establish success of the debiased confidence intervals when (among other assumptions) the initial estimate  $\widehat{\theta}_j$  is consistent in coordinate  $j$ . Our result is comparable with theirs (for a special choice of the penalty) but has the advantage of holding down to the Donoho-Tanner phase transition and permitting that taking  $\lambda$  be arbitrarily close to 0. We also do not require that  $\| \widehat{\pmb{\theta}} \|_0 / n \leq 1 / 2$  with high-probability.

The leave-one-out construction is a renormalized empirical correlation between the residuals of the regression of  $y_{\mathrm{init}}$  on  $X_{-j}$  and of  $x_{j}$  on  $X_{-j}$ . It is thus similar to a method proposed by [71, 65], in which the partial correlation between two features in a Gaussian graphical model is estimated by regressing each of these features on the remaining features. For each regression, [71, 65] use the scaled Lasso and must assume  $s = o(\sqrt{n})$  (up to logarithmic terms) to achieve normal inference. In contrast, we assume that one of the regressions — that of  $x_{j}$  on  $X_{-j}$  — is known perfectly, whereas the second regression — that of  $y_{\mathrm{init}}$  on  $X_{-j}$  — must be estimated and can have much less structure (possibly linear sparsity). For this reason, we require a degrees-of-freedom correction, which is not present in [71, 65].

Relation to the conditional randomization test. It is worth remarking that exact tests and confidence intervals for  $\theta_j^*$  may be constructed in our setting. In fact, when the feature distribution is known, one can perform an exact test of

$$
\boldsymbol {y} \perp \left. \check {\boldsymbol {x}} _ {j} \right| \boldsymbol {X} _ {- j}, \tag {30}
$$

even without Gaussianity or any assumption on the conditional distribution of the outcome  $\pmb{y}$  given the features  $\pmb{X}$  (see, e.g., [20, 48, 54]). The test which achieves this is called the conditional randomization test and is feasible to use for any arbitrary variable importance statistic  $T(\pmb{y},\pmb{X})$ . The key observation leading to the construction of the conditional randomization test is that under the null, the distribution of  $T(\pmb{y},\pmb{X})\mid \pmb{X}_{-j}$  is equal to the distribution of  $T(\pmb{y},\pmb{x}_j',\pmb{X}_{-j})\mid \pmb{X}_{-j}$  where  $\pmb{x}_j'$  is drawn by the statistician from the distribution  $\pmb{x}_j\mid \pmb{X}_{-j}$  without using  $\pmb{y}$ . Under the null, this distribution can be computed to arbitrary precision by

Monte Carlo sampling. We refer the reader to [20, 48, 54] for more details about how these observations lead to the construction of an exact test.

When the linear model is well-specified, the null (30) corresponds to  $\theta_{j}^{*} = 0$ , and our leave-one-out procedure with  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}} = 0$  implements the conditional randomization test under this null, as we now explain. The statistic  $\xi_{j}$ , defined in Eq. (27) and used in the construction of the leave-one-out interval, can also be used as the variable importance statistic in the conditional randomization test. The Gaussian design assumption and the choice of statistic  $\xi_{j}$  permit an explicit description of the null conditional distribution  $\xi_{j}|y,X_{-j}$ . Indeed, because  $\check{x}_j^\perp$  is independent of  $(y,X_{-j},\widehat{\theta}_{\mathrm{loo}})$  under the null  $\theta_j^* = 0$ , one has

$$
\sqrt {n} \xi_ {j} | \pmb {y}, \pmb {X} _ {- j} \sim \mathsf {N} \Big (0, \Sigma_ {j | - j} ^ {- 1} (\widehat {\tau} _ {\mathrm {l o o}} ^ {j}) ^ {2} \Big).
$$

In our setting, we can access the null conditional distribution through its analytic form rather than through Monte Carlo sampling. The test which rejects when  $0 \notin \mathrm{Cl}_j^{\mathrm{loo}}$  is exactly the conditional randomization test for the null (30) based on the variable importance statistic  $|\xi_j|$ . As a consequence, the leave-one-out confidence intervals have exact finite sample coverage under the null  $\theta_j^* = 0$  when  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}} = 0$ . Moreover, Theorem 12 provides more than what existing theory on the conditional randomization test can provide: it gives confidence intervals which are valid under proportional asymptotics and a power analysis for the corresponding tests.

The linearity assumption in our setting allows us to push this rationale further. When  $\theta_{j}^{*} = \widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}}$ , the  $j^{\mathrm{th}}$  residualized covariate  $\check{x}_j^\perp$  is independent of the pseudo-outcome  $\mathbf{y}_{\mathrm{init}}$  and  $\mathbf{X}_{-j}$ . Thus, by the same logic as above, the leave-one-out confidence interval achieve exact coverage when  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}} = \theta_j^*$ . In particular, we have an exactly valid test of  $\theta_{j}^{*} = \widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}}$  for all values of  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}}$ . The inversion of this collection of tests, indexed by  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}}$ , produces an exact confidence interval. Details of this construction are provided in Appendix B.8.

We prefer the approximate interval  $\mathsf{Cl}_j^{\mathrm{loo}}$  to the exact interval outlined in the preceding paragraph for computational reasons. The construction of these exact confidence intervals requires recomputing the leave-one-out Lasso estimate using pseudo-outcome  $y - \check{x}_j^\perp \widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}}$  for each value of  $\widehat{\theta}_{j,\mathrm{init}}$ . In contrast, the leave-one-out confidence interval we provide requires only computing a single leave-one-out Lasso estimate. It achieves only approximate coverage, but our simulations in Section 5.2 show that coverage is good already for  $n,p,s$  on the order of 10s or 100s. An additional benefit of Theorem 12 is its quantification of the length of the leave-one-out confidence intervals and the power of the corresponding tests, which are not in general accessible for the conditional randomization test or confidence intervals based on it. In fact, because the test  $0\notin \mathsf{Cl}_j^{\mathrm{loo}}$  is exactly the conditional randomization test, Theorem 12(a) applied under  $\theta_j^*$  provides an estimate of the power of the conditional randomization test under alternative  $\theta_j^* = \omega$ .

4.7. Restricted eigenvalues and the Donoho-Tanner phase transition. An important feature of our results is that they hold down to the Donoho-Tanner phase transition, which can be weaker than the requirement based on restricted eigenvalue conditions.

Specifically, the standard restricted eigenvalue on support  $S \subset [p]$  of a matrix  $\mathbf{X} \in \mathbb{R}^{n \times p}$  is defined as (see, for example, [15, 9])

$$
\operatorname {R E} (S, c) := \operatorname {R E} (S, c; \boldsymbol {X}) := \min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta} \in \mathcal {C} _ {\mathrm {R E}} (s, c)} \frac {1}{\sqrt {n}} \| \boldsymbol {X} \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {2} > 0,
$$

where  $\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{RE}}(S,c) \coloneqq \{\pmb{\theta} \in \mathbb{R}^p : \| \pmb{\theta}_{S^c}\|_1 \leq c\|\pmb{\theta}_S\|_1, \| \pmb{\theta}\|_2 = 1\}$ . In order for bounds based on restricted eigenvalues to yield the correct estimation error rate, one typically needs  $\mathrm{RE}(S,c)$  to be bounded away from zero for some  $c$  strictly larger than 1.

In the random design setting of the present paper, we illustrate by the following example that,  $\mathrm{RE}(S,c) = 0$  with high-probability for some non-vanishing interval of sampling rates above the Donoho-Tanner phase transition.

PROPOSITION 13. Consider a block diagonal matrix  $\pmb{\Sigma} \in \mathbb{R}^{p \times p}$  whose first  $s/2$  diagonal blocks are  $\pmb{K} = \begin{pmatrix} 1 & \rho \\ \rho & 1 \end{pmatrix}$  for some constant  $\rho > 0$ , and whose lower right  $(p - s) \times (p - s)$  diagonal block is  $\pmb{I}_{p - s}$ . Let  $S = \{1, 2, \dots, s\}$  and  $\pmb{x}^* = \mathbf{1}_S \in \mathbb{R}^p$  be the indicator vector on  $S$ .

Consider the limit  $s, p, n \to \infty$  with  $s/p = \varepsilon$  and  $n/p = \delta$  fixed. In this setting, the Gaussian width  $\mathcal{G}(\boldsymbol{x}^*, \boldsymbol{\Sigma}) = \overline{\mathcal{G}}(\varepsilon, \delta, \rho) \in (0, \infty)$  only depends on  $n, p, s$  through the ratios  $\varepsilon, \delta$ . Further, there exists  $\Delta(\varepsilon, \delta, \rho) > 0$  such that if  $\mathcal{G}(\boldsymbol{x}^*, \boldsymbol{\Sigma})^2 < \delta < \mathcal{G}(\boldsymbol{x}^*, \boldsymbol{\Sigma})^2 + \Delta(\varepsilon, \delta, \rho)$ , then with probability going to 1 as  $p \to \infty$ ,  $\mathrm{RE}(S, c; \boldsymbol{X}) = 0$  for all  $c \geq 1$ .

We prove Proposition 13 in Appendix C.4. We remark that the set  $\mathcal{C}_{\mathrm{RE}}(S,1)$  is closely related to the cone  $\mathcal{K}(\pmb{x},\pmb{\Sigma})$  used in defining the Gaussian width  $\mathcal{G}(\pmb{x},\pmb{\Sigma})$ : the former is based on the cone constraint  $\| \pmb{\theta}_{S^c}\|_1 \leq \| \pmb{\theta}_S\|_1$ , whereas the latter is based on the cone constraint  $\| \pmb{\theta}_{S^c}\|_1 \leq \langle \mathrm{sign}(\pmb{x}),\pmb{\theta} \rangle$ , where  $S = \mathrm{supp}(\pmb{x})$ . The right-hand side  $\| \pmb{\theta}_S\|_1$  is the supremum of  $\langle \mathrm{sign}(\pmb{x}),\pmb{\theta} \rangle$  over all sign vectors  $\pmb{x}$  with support  $S$ . Existing proofs based on the restricted eigenvalue condition [15, 9] go through if  $\| \pmb{\theta}_S\|_1$  were replaced by  $\langle \mathrm{sign}(\pmb{x}),\pmb{\theta} \rangle$  in the definition of the restricted eigenvalue condition (indeed, in these proofs, this quantity serves only as a bound on  $\| \pmb{\theta}_S^* \|_1 - \| \pmb{\theta}_S\|_1$ ). Thus, Proposition 13 as demonstrates the importance of using  $\langle \mathrm{sign}(\pmb{x}),\pmb{\theta} \rangle$  instead of  $\| \pmb{\theta}_S\|_1$  in definitions of the Gaussian width or restricted eigenvalue rather than demonstrating a fundamental limitation of prior analyses. A fundamental improvement of our analysis relative to prior analyses is that we can take  $c = 1$  rather than  $c > 1$ . For fixed  $c > 1$ , even a modified restricted eigenvalue condition using  $\langle \mathrm{sign}(\pmb{x}),\pmb{\theta} \rangle$  results in a gap with respect to our condition  $\mathcal{G}(\pmb{x}^*,\pmb{\Sigma})^2 < \delta$ .

A natural question is whether our results hold for sampling rates below the Donoho-Tanner phase transition. The following proposition gives a partial answer, in the negative direction.

PROPOSITION 14. Consider  $\pmb{x} \in \{-1,0,1\}^p$  with  $\| \pmb{x}\|_0 \geq 1$  and  $\epsilon > 0$ . If

$$
\mathcal {G} (\boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) \geq \sqrt {\frac {n}{p}} + \epsilon ,
$$

then, for any  $r > 0$ , there exists  $\pmb{\theta}^*$  (depending on  $r, \lambda, \sigma, \kappa_{\min}, \kappa_{\max}, n, p$ , and  $\| \pmb{x} \|_0$ ) with  $\mathrm{sign}(\pmb{\theta}^*) = \pmb{x}$  such that if the data is generated according to (2), then

$$
\mathbb {P} \left(\left\| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} - \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \right\| _ {2} \geq r\right) \geq 1 - C e ^ {- c p \epsilon^ {2}},
$$

where  $C, c > 0$  depend only on  $\kappa_{\max}$ .

In particular, the Lasso has unbounded risk on sparse balls below the Donoho-Tanner phase transition, whence Theorem 5 cannot hold with bounded fixed-point parameters. We prove Proposition 14 in Section C.1.

5. Numerical simulations. This section contains numerical experiments which  $(i)$  illustrate the success of the degrees-of-adjustment for  $n,p,s$  in the 100s to 1000s,  $(ii)$  compare the leave-one-out and debiased confidence intervals, and  $(iii)$  support the expectation that our results may hold for a more general class of feature distributions than Gaussian. We present here some representative simulations and refer to Appendix D for further results.

5.1. Debiasing with degrees-of-freedom adjustment. We compare the degrees-of-freedom adjusted debiased Lasso of Eq. (4) with the unadjusted estimator of Eq. (23).

Figure 1 reports results on the distribution of the two estimators. We set  $p = 1000$ ,  $n = 500$  and  $s = 200$ , and fix  $\theta^{*} \in \mathbb{R}^{p}$  with  $s / 2$  coordinates equal to  $10 / \sqrt{s}$  and the rest equal to  $-10 / \sqrt{s}$  chosen uniformly at random. We repeat the following steps  $N_{\mathrm{sim}} = 500$  times. First, we generate data from the linear model (2) where  $x_{i} \sim \mathsf{N}(\mathbf{0}, \mathbf{\Sigma})$ ,  $\sigma = 1$  and  $\mathbf{\Sigma}$  comes from the autoregressive model AR(0.5):

$$
\Sigma_ {i j} = 0. 5 ^ {| i - j |}.
$$

In each simulation, we keep the same  $\theta^{*}$  vector but independent draws of  $X$ ,  $z$ . We compute for each  $j \leq p$  the standardized values  $\frac{\sqrt{n} \Sigma_{j| - j}^{1/2} (1 - \| \widehat{\theta} \|_0 / n) (\widehat{\theta}_j^{\mathrm{d}} - \theta_j^*)}{\| y - X \widehat{\theta} \|_2 / \sqrt{n}}$  and  $\frac{\sqrt{n} \Sigma_{j| - j}^{1/2} (\widehat{\theta}_{0j}^{\mathrm{d}} - \theta_j^*)}{\| y - X \widehat{\theta} \|_2 / \sqrt{n}}$  corresponding to the debiased Lasso with and without degrees-of-freedom adjustment respectively. Aggregating over coordinates and simulations (giving  $p \cdot N_{\mathrm{sim}} = 500,000$  observations of single coordinates), we plot histograms and quantile plots for all coordinates corresponding to  $\theta_j = -10 / \sqrt{s}$ ,  $0$ ,  $10 / \sqrt{s}$  separately. In the quantile plots, the empirical quantiles are compared with the theoretical quantiles of the standard normal distribution  $\mathsf{N}(0,1)$ .

Without the degrees-of-freedom adjustment, visible deviations from normality occur. For active coordinates, we observe bias and skew; for inactive coordinates, we observe tails which are too fat. The fattening of the tails occurs around and beyond the quantiles corresponding to two-sided confidence intervals constructed at the 0.05 level. Thus, failure to implement degrees-of-freedom adjustments will lead to under-coverage in standard statistical practice even prior to corrections for multiple testing. In contrast, with degrees-of-freedom adjustment, no obvious deviations from normality occur for either the inactive or active coordinates. Normality is retained well into the normal tail. Our simulations are well into the proportional regime. In agreement with [44, 13, 12, 56], these simulations confirm that the degrees-of-freedom adjustment suffices to recover normality.

The simulations presented in Figure 1 are representative of simulations conducted at differing sample sizes, sparsity levels, and correlation parameters  $\rho$ . We present these simulations in Appendix D. We also present simulations for equicorrelated designs.

5.2. Confidence interval for a single coordinate. In this section, we consider the behavior of the debiased confidence interval  $\mathsf{Cl}_j^{\mathrm{d}}$  (defined in Eq. (22)) and leave-one-out confidence interval  $\mathsf{Cl}_j^{\mathrm{loo}}$  (defined in Eq. (28)).

In Figure 3, we examine the coverage of the confidence interval for both an active coordinate and an inactive coordinate. We consider  $p = 100$ ,  $n = 25$ , and  $s = 20$ , and fix  $\pmb{\theta}^{*} \in \mathbb{R}^{p}$  with  $s / 2$  coordinates equal to  $10 / \sqrt{s}$  and the rest equal to  $-10 / \sqrt{s}$ . The locations of the active coordinates are chosen uniformly at random. We set the coordinate of interest to be  $\theta_{50}$ . For each model specification, we perform the following  $N_{\mathrm{sim}} = 1000$  times. First, we generate data from the linear model (2) with  $\sigma = 1$  and  $\Sigma$  the AR(0.5) covariance  $\Sigma_{ij} = 0.5^{|i - j|}$ . We construct for  $j = 50$  the  $(1 - \alpha)$ -confidence intervals  $\mathsf{CI}_j^{\mathrm{d}}$  and  $\mathsf{CI}_j^{\mathrm{loo}}$  at level  $\alpha = 0.05$ . We also construct the following interval based on the debiased Lasso without degrees-of-freedom adjustment given by Eq. (23):

$$
\mathsf {C l} _ {j} ^ {\mathrm {d , n o D O F}} := \left[ \widehat {\theta} _ {0 j} ^ {\mathrm {d}} - \frac {\Sigma_ {j | - j} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| \pmb {y} - \pmb {X} \widehat {\pmb {\theta}} \| _ {2}}{n} z _ {1 - \alpha / 2}, \widehat {\theta} _ {0 j} ^ {\mathrm {d}} + \frac {\Sigma_ {j | - j} ^ {- 1 / 2} \| \pmb {y} - \pmb {X} \widehat {\pmb {\theta}} \| _ {2}}{n} z _ {1 - \alpha / 2} \right].
$$

The confidence intervals from the first 40 of the 1000 simulations are plotted in Figure 3 for the cases  $\theta_{50}^{*} = 0$  and  $\theta_{50}^{*} = 10 / \sqrt{s} \approx 2.24$ . Both the debiased Lasso and the leave-one-out confidence intervals achieve coverage. Although in some simulations there appears to be

a small difference between the intervals computed by the two methods, in most cases the confidence intervals almost exactly agree. In contrast, when  $\theta_{50}^{*} = 10 / \sqrt{s}$ , the confidence interval without degrees-of-freedom adjustment is uncentered and too narrow, leading to large under-coverage. When  $\theta_{50}^{*} = 0$ , the empirical coverage (for 1000 simulations) is  $95.5\%$  for the debiased Lasso with degrees-of-freedom adjustment,  $95.2\%$  for leave-one-out confidence interval, and  $90.05\%$  for the debiased Lasso without degrees-of-freedom adjustment. When  $\theta_{50}^{*} = 10 / \sqrt{s}$ , these coverages are  $94.3\%$ ,  $93.9\%$ , and  $36.8\%$ , respectively. Note that confidence interval with degrees of freedom adjustment is undefined when  $\|\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}\|_0 = n$ . Because we take  $s/n$  very large, this occurs in some of our simulations. When this occurs, we count this as an non-coverage event, and omit to draw the confidence interval in our plots.

![](images/99a715d80f34760efd5cfcf519b1f7ed727118b6e7273d898127d49ff4a42868.jpg)

Fig 3. Confidence interval for a single coordinate  $\theta_{50}^{*}$ . Here  $p = 100$ ,  $n = 25$ ,  $s = 20$ ,  $\Sigma_{ij} = 0.5^{|i - j|}$ ,  $\lambda = 4$ ,  $\sigma = 1$ . In the top plots, the truth is  $\theta_{50}^{*} = 0$ , and in the bottom plots the truth is  $\theta_{50}^{*} = 10 / \sqrt{s} \approx 2.24$ .  
![](images/0ebb322a9a0aec38efa52709d5d7c891148eb3bc1a6d4c9e77e53da59add998c.jpg)  
Method — Cl<sub>1</sub> — Cl<sub>1</sub> <sup>100</sup> — Cl<sub>1</sub> <sup>d, no DOF</sup>

![](images/0c34ce44b0e68077cc470de032e4c41dd93637796954368302a66b1c4cf5c343.jpg)

These simulations provide evidence that the leave-one-out confidence intervals  $\mathsf{Cl}_j^{\mathrm{loo}}$  are valid for fixed coordinate  $j$ , already for moderate values of  $n,p$ . In this case, the debiased confidence intervals  $\mathsf{Cl}_j^{\mathrm{d}}$  appear to achieve coverage per-coordinate and not only on average across coordinates. Moreover, in this case, the confidence intervals and  $\mathsf{Cl}_j^{\mathrm{d}}$  and  $\mathsf{Cl}_j^{\mathrm{loo}}$  appear to be nearly equivalent.

We also consider the simulation set-up in Figure 2, in which the effective noise  $\widehat{\tau}$  for the debiased confidence intervals is too small. In the same simulations used to generate Figure 2, we display in the left plot of Figure 4 40 realizations of the debiased confidence interval with degrees of freedom adjustment and the leave-one-out confidence intervals. The empirical coverage across these 5000 replications was  $89.78\%$  for the debiased confidence intervals and  $94.78\%$  for the leave-one-out confidence intervals.

As expected, the debiased confidence intervals with width computed based on  $\widehat{\tau}$  are too narrow and undercover, whereas the leave-one-out confidence intervals are correctly calibrated and achieve coverage. Perhaps surprisingly, this occurs even though the debiased confidence intervals are wider than the leave-one-out confidence intervals. Indeed, across 5000 replications, the average value of  $\widehat{\tau} / \sqrt{1 - \rho^2}$  was 2.97 (1.6e-3) and  $\widehat{\tau}_{\mathrm{loo}}^{1}$  was 2.88 (1.5e-3), which gives a  $p$ -value for a non-zero difference in means of 2.2e-16 and a  $95\%$  confidence interval for the difference in means of [.080, .088]. This discrepancy is also visually apparent in the right plot of Figure 4, in which the debiased confidence intervals tend to be wider. If the correct standard error of [12] were used for the debiased Lasso, so that the intervals would achieve coverage, these intervals would be wider still. On the right plot of Figure 4, we display histograms of the test statistic  $\widehat{\theta}_1^{\mathrm{d}}$  and  $\xi_{j}$  across 5000 replications. We see that the leave-one-out confidence interval's test statistic has smaller variance than the debaised Lasso test statistic, indicative of the fact that in this case we may achieve more precise inference with the leave-one-out construction than the debiased construction.

![](images/2b0a102de9bc5173754dcf717c10554e6cd6f94493659cef640e04a9d0076b95.jpg)  
Fig 4. The debiased Lasso and leave-one-out confidence intervals for  $p = 1000$ ,  $n = 500$ ,  $s = 100$ ,  $\rho^2 = .5$ ,  $\sigma = 1$ ,  $\lambda = \sqrt{2\sigma\log(p / s) / n} = .096$ ,  $\pmb{\theta}^{*} = 3\sqrt{s}\lambda \pmb{v}$ , where  $\pmb{v} = (0, \mathbf{1}_s, \mathbf{0}_{p - s - 1}) / \sqrt{s}$  and  $\pmb{\Sigma} = \mathbf{I}_p + \rho \pmb{e}_1\pmb{v}^\top + \rho \pmb{v}\pmb{e}_1^\top$ . On the left, we plot the debiased confidence interval  $\mathsf{Cl}_1^{\mathrm{d}}$  and leave-one-out confidence intervals  $\mathsf{Cl}_1^{\mathrm{loo}}$  for  $\theta_1^* = 0$ . On the right, we plot histograms of  $\widehat{\theta}_1^{\mathrm{d}}$  and  $\xi_{1}$  across 5000 replications.

![](images/6bbcf14c8341236114f67b7e0047f95bc0717e0f4b66f56d48a519262ebfb0a5.jpg)

5.3. Non-Gaussian designs. The results described in this work are proven under correlated Gaussian designs. When covariates are independent, numerical simulations and universality arguments in previous work suggest exact asymptotic characterizations still hold for independent but possibly non-Gaussian covariates (see e.g. [6, 61, 57] for rigorous universality results). Moreover, such universality phenomena are also expected to hold beyond

the linear models: for instance, [72] (in Figure 9) present simulations for logistic regression with independent but non-Gaussian covariates whose behavior agrees with the corresponding asymptotic predictions for independent Gaussian covariates.

Here we provide some numerical evidence which suggests that our theory describes the behavior of the Lasso under some realistic data generating distributions (when the Gaussianity assumption breaks). We consider the design matrix with covariates generated according to a hidden Markov model. Hidden Markov models are frequently used for modeling the covariates in genetics applications (see, e.g. [68]). The specification of the hidden Markov model used in our simulation is described in details in Appendix D. The model is such that covariates with indices differing by approximately 10 or less have non-negligible correlation. The response is generated according to model (2), with  $n = 1280$ ,  $p = 2000$ ,  $s = 0.128p$ , and  $\sigma = 0.2$ , and all active coordinates of  $\theta^*$  are set to 1. We run our debiasing procedure with degrees of freedom adjustment for  $N_{\mathrm{sim}} = 10$  independent realizations of the data, with the knowledge of the underlying covariance matrix for the covariates. We then aggregate the standardized and centered debiased Lasso estimates across coordinates and across simulations, separately for the inactive and active coordinates, and provide a qq-plot for each; the results are presented in Figure 5. It is worth noting that from the simulations, one can see the success of the debiasing procedure with degrees of freedom adjustment carries even into the tails of the distribution. This phenomenon cannot be justified using prior theory based on independent Gaussian covariates.

![](images/3a4bbe74c27a9804e24d7c1701cff7cf8b3a4769439b6eabe57e849f6c3ba612.jpg)  
Fig 5. The debiased Lasso with and without degrees-of-freedom (DOF) adjustment for hidden Markov model features. Here  $n = 1280$ ,  $p = 2000$ ,  $s = .128 \cdot p$ , and  $\sigma = .2$ , and all active coordinates of  $\theta^*$  equal to 1. Quantiles and densities are compared with the ones of the standard normal distribution.

6. Main proof ingredients. Our proofs are built upon a tight version of Gordon's min-max theorem for convex functions. Gordon's original theorem [38, 39] is a Gaussian comparison inequality for the minimization-maximization of two related Gaussian processes, and has several applications in random matrix theory and convex optimization [67, 63]. In a line of work initiated by [69] and formalized by [74], the comparison inequality was shown to be tight when the underlying Gaussian process is convex-concave. This observation has led to several works establishing exact asymptotics for high-dimensional convex procedures, including general penalized M-estimators in linear regression [74, 73] and binary classification [26, 58, 53]. (We also refer to [7, 2, 27, 34, 64, 3] for alternative proof techniques to obtain sharp results in high-dimensional regression models, in the proportional asymptotics.)

Earlier work has so far focused on the case of independent features or correlated features with unpenalized or ridge-penalized procedures. Analyzing the Lasso estimator under general Gaussian designs, however, requires overcoming several technical challenges, as the  $\ell_1$ -penalty breaks the isometry underlying the procedure. In this section, we summarize our proof strategy, emphasizing the technical innovations that are required in the context of general correlated designs. Our work builds on the approach of [56], which studied the Lasso and debiased Lasso estimators in the case  $\pmb{\Sigma} = \mathbf{I}_p$ .

Control of the Lasso estimate. We find it useful to first rewrite the Lasso optimization objective as

$$
\mathcal {C} (\boldsymbol {v}) := \frac {1}{2 n} \| \sigma \boldsymbol {z} - \boldsymbol {X} \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \frac {\lambda}{\sqrt {n}} \left(\| \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} + \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {1} - \| \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \| _ {1}\right). \tag {31}
$$

Here we introduce the prediction error vector  $\pmb{v} \coloneqq \pmb{\Sigma}^{1/2} (\pmb{\theta} - \pmb{\theta}^*)$ . The variable  $\pmb{v}$  is used to whiten the design matrix and isolate the dependence of the objective on it. Indeed,  $X \pmb{\Sigma}^{-1/2}$  has entries distributed i.i.d. from  $\mathsf{N}(0, 1)$ , and we have expanded  $\pmb{y}$  to reveal its dependence on  $\pmb{X}$ . We denote by  $\widehat{\pmb{v}}$  the minimizer of  $\mathcal{C}(\pmb{v})$ , i.e.,  $\widehat{\pmb{v}} \coloneqq \pmb{\Sigma}^{1/2} (\widehat{\pmb{\theta}} - \pmb{\theta}^*)$ . By a standard argument, Gordon's min-max theorem implies that the Lasso optimization behaves, in a certain sense, like the optimization of the simpler objective

$$
\mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {v}) := \frac {1}{2} \left(\sqrt {\sigma^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2} ^ {2}} - \frac {\langle \boldsymbol {g} , \boldsymbol {v} \rangle}{\sqrt {n}}\right) _ {+} ^ {2} + \frac {\lambda}{\sqrt {n}} \left(\| \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} + \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {1} - \| \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \| _ {1}\right), \tag {32}
$$

which we call Gordon's objective. The precise statement is as follows.

LEMMA 6.1 (Gordon's lemma). The following statements hold true.

(a) Let  $D\subset \mathbb{R}^p$  be a closed set. For all  $t\in \mathbb{R}$

$$
\mathbb {P} \left(\min  _ {\boldsymbol {v} \in D} \mathcal {C} (\boldsymbol {v}) \leq t\right) \leq 2 \mathbb {P} \left(\min  _ {\boldsymbol {v} \in D} \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {v}) \leq t\right).
$$

(b) Let  $D\subset \mathbb{R}^p$  be a closed, convex set. For all  $t\in \mathbb{R}$

$$
\mathbb {P} \left(\min  _ {\boldsymbol {v} \in D} \mathcal {C} (\boldsymbol {v}) \geq t\right) \leq 2 \mathbb {P} \left(\min  _ {\boldsymbol {v} \in D} \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {v}) \geq t\right).
$$

By studying Gordon's objective, and comparing the value of  $\min_{\boldsymbol{v} \in D} \mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol{v})$  for suitable choices of the set  $D$ , we can extract properties of  $\widehat{\boldsymbol{v}}$  and hence  $\widehat{\boldsymbol{\theta}}$ . In particular, in Theorem 5, we compare the value taken for  $D = \mathbb{R}^p$  and

$$
D = \left\{\boldsymbol {\theta} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p} \mid \left| \phi (\boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} + \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {v}) - \mathbb {E} [ \phi (\widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {f}) ] \right| > \epsilon \right\},
$$

where  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}^f$  is defined by Eq. (6) with  $\tau^{*},\zeta^{*}$  the unique solution to Eqs. (9a) and (9b). The argument is carried out in detail in Appendix B.1.

This discussion clarifies that we can control the behavior of the Lasso objective only insofar as we can control the behavior of Gordon's objective. The major technical challenge to apply this approach to general correlated designs is in relating the minimizer of Gordon's objective to the fixed design estimator  $\widehat{\theta}^f$ . In particular, this requires showing that the solution  $(\tau^{*},\zeta^{*})$  of Eqs. (9a) and (9b) is unique and bounded in terms of simple model parameters (see Lemma 4).

Although several parts of our argument are similar to the arguments of [56], establishing existence, uniqueness, and boundedness of  $\tau^{*},\zeta^{*}$  requires entirely new techniques. Generalizing an idea introduced in [58], we control the solutions Eqs. (9a) and (9b) by showing that

these equations are the KKT conditions for a certain convex optimization problem on the infinite dimensional Hilbert space  $L^2(\mathbb{R}^p; \mathbb{R}^p)$ . To be more specific, the optimization problem is

$$
\min  _ {\boldsymbol {v} \in L ^ {2}} \mathcal {E} (\boldsymbol {v}) := \min  _ {\boldsymbol {v} \in L ^ {2}} \left\{\frac {1}{2} \left(\sqrt {\| \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} + \sigma^ {2}} - \frac {\langle \boldsymbol {g} , \boldsymbol {v} \rangle_ {L ^ {2}}}{\sqrt {n}}\right) _ {+} ^ {2} + \frac {\lambda}{\sqrt {n}} \mathbb {E} \left[ \| \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} + \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {v} (\boldsymbol {g}) \| _ {1} - \| \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \| _ {1} \right] \right\}.
$$

The objectives  $\mathcal{L}$  and  $\mathcal{E}$  are closely related, but their arguments belong to different spaces. The objective  $\mathcal{L}$  takes vectorial arguments  $\pmb{v} \in \mathbb{R}^p$ ; the objective  $\mathcal{E}$  takes functional arguments  $\pmb{v}: \mathbb{R}^p \to \mathbb{R}^p$ . Both objectives are convex. In Appendix A.4, we show that  $\pmb{v} \in L^2$  is a minimizer of  $\mathcal{E}$  if and only if  $\pmb{v}(\pmb{g}) = \eta (\pmb{\Sigma}^{1/2} \pmb{\theta}^* + \tau^* \pmb{g}; \zeta^*)$  for  $\tau^*$ ,  $\zeta^*$  a solution to the fixed point equations. This follows from showing that Eqs. (9a) and (9b) correspond to KKT conditions for the minimization of  $\mathcal{E}$ . Further, we show that  $\mathcal{E}$  diverges to infinity as  $\| \pmb{v} \|_{L^2} \to \infty$  and is strictly convex in a neighborhood of any minimizer, whence a minimizer exists, and it is unique. We are then able to conclude that the fixed point equations also have a unique solution. We defer the details of this argument to Appendix A.4.

Controlling the size of the fixed point parameters relies on bounding the norm of the minimizer of  $\mathcal{E}$ . Again, our approach is geometric: rather than analyzing the fixed point equations directly, we study the growth of the objective  $\mathcal{E}$  as  $\| \pmb{v} \|_{L^2}$  diverges. The functional Gaussian width (13) controls this growth. This explains the centrality of the Gaussian width  $\mathcal{G}(\pmb{x}, \pmb{\Sigma})$  in our analysis. In fact, under only a sparsity constraint on  $\pmb{\theta}^{*}$ , we can control the growth  $\mathcal{E}$  in  $\| \pmb{v} \|_{L^2}$  in an  $n$ -independent way only when  $\mathcal{G}(\pmb{x}, \pmb{\Sigma}) < \sqrt{\delta}$  where  $\pmb{x} \in \partial \| \pmb{\theta}^{*} \|_1$  (see, also, the proof of Proposition 14). The detailed argument bounding the fixed point parameters is in Appendix A.4.

The present approach is significantly more general both than the one of [56], which studies the Lasso for  $\pmb{\Sigma} = \mathbf{I}_p$ , and of [58] which studies binary classification under a ridge-type regularization. When  $\pmb{\Sigma} = \mathbf{I}_p$ , the Lasso estimator in the fixed-design model is separable, and Eqs. (9a) and (9b) simplify because

$$
\mathsf {R} (\tau^ {2}, \zeta) = \mathbb {E} _ {\Theta , G} [ (\eta_ {\mathrm {s o f t}} (\Theta^ {*} + \tau G, \lambda / \zeta) - \Theta^ {*}) ^ {2} ],
$$

$$
\frac {\mathrm {d f} (\tau^ {2} , \zeta)}{p} = \mathbb {P} \left(\eta_ {\text {s o f t}} \left(\Theta^ {*} + \tau G, \lambda / \zeta\right) \neq 0\right),
$$

where  $\Theta^{*} \sim \frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\delta_{\sqrt{n}\theta_{j}^{*}}$  independent of  $G \sim \mathsf{N}(0,1)$ , and  $\eta_{\mathrm{soft}}(y;\zeta) \coloneqq (|y| - \zeta)_{+}\mathrm{sign}(y)$ . Hence — in that case — existence and uniqueness of the solution of Eqs. (9a) and (9b) can be proved by analyzing the explicit form of these equations.

Also, our approach is more general than the one of [58], which constructs a Hilbert-space optimization problem by taking the  $n,p\to \infty$  limit of the Gordon's problem. In the present case, since we intend to establish a non-asymptotic control, for finite  $n,p$  there is no natural sequence of covariances in which to embed  $\pmb{\Sigma}$ .

Control of the Lasso sparsity. It is not feasible to directly control quantity  $\| \widehat{\pmb{\theta}}\| _0 / n$  using Theorem 5 with  $\phi (\pmb {\theta}) = \| \pmb {\theta}\| _0 / n$  because this function is not Lipschitz or even continuous. Instead, we establish lower and upper bounds on the sparsity separately.

To explain the argument, define

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {t}} = \frac {1}{\lambda \sqrt {n}} \boldsymbol {X} ^ {\top} (\boldsymbol {y} - \boldsymbol {X} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}}),
$$

and observe that by the KKT conditions for Eq. (1),  $\widehat{\pmb{t}}\in \partial \| \widehat{\pmb{\theta}}\| _1$ . Define for any  $\pmb {\theta}\in \mathbb{R}^p$  the  $\epsilon$ -strongly active coordinates of  $\pmb{\theta}$  to be  $\{j\in [p]\mid |\theta_j| > \epsilon /\sqrt{n}\}$ . Likewise, for any  $\pmb {t}\in \mathbb{R}^{p}$  define the  $\epsilon$ -strongly inactive coordinates of  $\pmb{t}$  to be  $\{j\in [p]\mid |t_j| < 1 - \epsilon \}$  (this definition

is motivated by the fact that if  $t$  is the sub-gradient of the Lasso, if  $|t_j| < 1 - \epsilon$  then  $\theta_j = 0$  and  $t_j$  would have to change by at least  $\epsilon$  for  $\theta_j$  to become active). Our argument relies on the following two facts (here  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}$  is, as always, the Lasso estimate, and  $\widehat{t}$  is the subgradient of Eq. (72)):

(33)

$$
\text {i f} \frac {\| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \| _ {0}}{n} \leq 1 - \zeta^ {*} - \epsilon , \text {t h e n} \inf  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \left\{\| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} - \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {2} \left| \frac {| \{j | | \theta_ {j} | > \epsilon / \sqrt {n} \} |}{n} > 1 - \zeta^ {*} - \frac {\epsilon}{2} \right. \right\} \geq \sqrt {\frac {\epsilon^ {3}}{2}},
$$

and

(34)

$$
\text {i f} \frac {\| \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} \| _ {0}}{n} \geq 1 - \zeta^ {*} + \epsilon , \text {t h e n} \inf  _ {t} \left\{\| \widehat {\boldsymbol {t}} - \boldsymbol {t} \| _ {2} \left| \frac {\left| \left\{j \mid | t _ {j} | <   1 - \epsilon \right\} \right|}{n} > 1 - \zeta^ {*} - \frac {\epsilon}{2} \right. \right\} \geq \sqrt {\frac {\epsilon^ {3}}{2}}.
$$

The first implication holds because the vectors  $\theta$  and  $\widehat{\theta}$  differ by at least  $\epsilon/\sqrt{n}$  in  $n\epsilon/2$  coordinates; namely, in those coordinates in which  $\theta$  is  $\epsilon$ -strongly active and  $\widehat{\theta}$  is inactive. The second implication holds similarly. In words, vectors which are very sparse are separated in Euclidean distance from vectors with many  $\epsilon$ -active coordinates; similarly, subgradients with many active coordinates are separated in Euclidean distance from vectors with many  $\epsilon$ -inactive coordinates.

To proceed, we leverage the following fact: for any set  $D \subset \mathbb{R}^p$  which contains the fixed-design Lasso estimate  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}^f$  with high-probability, the random design Lasso estimate  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}$  is close to  $D$  with high-probability. Similarly, for any set  $D \subset \mathbb{R}^p$  which contains the fixed-design subgradient  $\widehat{\pmb{t}}^f$  with high-probability, the random-design subgradient  $\widehat{\pmb{t}}$  is close to  $D$  with high-probability. We provide control of the subgradient which is analogous to the control we provide of the Lasso estimate in Lemma 16 of the appendices. A similar statement holds for the Lasso estimate, and developed in the proof of Theorem 5. Taking  $D$  to be the set over which the infimum in Eq. (33) (resp. Eq. (34)) is taken, we can conclude  $\| \widehat{\pmb{\theta}} \|_0 / n > 1 - \zeta^* - \epsilon$  (resp.  $\| \widehat{\pmb{\theta}} \|_0 / n < 1 - \zeta^* + \epsilon$ ) with high-probability as soon as we can show  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}^f \in D$  (resp.  $\widehat{\pmb{t}}^f \in D$ ) with high-probability. The details of this argument are carried out in Appendix B.5.

Control of the debiased Lasso. We may write the debiased Lasso as a function of the Lasso estimate  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}$ , the subgradient  $\widehat{t}$ , and the Lasso sparsity  $\| \widehat{\pmb{\theta}} \|_0 / n$ :

$$
\widehat {\pmb {\theta}} ^ {\mathrm {d}} = \widehat {\pmb {\theta}} + \frac {\lambda \pmb {\Sigma} ^ {- 1} \widehat {\pmb {t}} / \sqrt {n}}{1 - \| \widehat {\pmb {\theta}} \| _ {0} / n}.
$$

Because  $1 - \|\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}\|_0 / n$  concentrates on  $\zeta^*$  by Theorem 8, the debiased Lasso is with high-probability close to

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} + \frac {\lambda \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {- 1} \widehat {\boldsymbol {t}} / \sqrt {n}}{\zeta^ {*}}. \tag {35}
$$

Our goal is to show that  $\widehat{\theta} +\frac{\lambda\Sigma^{-1}\widehat{t} / \sqrt{n}}{\zeta^{*}} -\theta^{*}$  is approximately Gaussian noise with zero mean and covariance  $\tau^{*2}\Sigma^{-1}$ . Heuristically, if we replace the Lasso estimate and subgradient by their fixed-design counterparts, we get

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {f} + \frac {\lambda \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {- 1} \widehat {\boldsymbol {t}} ^ {f} / \sqrt {n}}{\zeta^ {*}} - \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} = \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {f} - \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} + \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {1 / 2} (\boldsymbol {y} ^ {f} - \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} ^ {f}) = \tau^ {*} \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {- 1 / 2} \boldsymbol {g} / \sqrt {n},
$$

where in the first inequality we have used that  $\frac{\lambda}{\sqrt{n}\zeta^{*}}\widehat{t}^{f} = \Sigma^{1 / 2}(\pmb{y}^{f} - \Sigma^{1 / 2}\widehat{\pmb{\theta}})$  by the KKT conditions for the optimization (6). Thus, we would like to justify the heuristic replacement of the random design quantities with their fixed-design counterparts.

It turns out that it is not straightforward to justify this heuristic, and here again we require an entirely new arugment compared to that which appears in [56]. The challenge is as follows: Theorem 5 and Lemma 16 compare the distributions of  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}$  and  $\widehat{t}$  to their fixed design counterparts individually, but does not say anything about their joint distribution. The paper [56] addresses this challenge by providing a simple characterization of  $\hat{\mu} = \frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\delta_{\sqrt{n}\widehat{\theta}_j}$  and  $\hat{\mu}' := \frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\delta_{\widehat{t}_j}$ , and arguing that their is only one joint distribution  $\frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\delta_{\sqrt{n}\widehat{\theta}_j,\widehat{t}_j}$  which is consistent with the Lasso KKT conditions and is consistent with the marginal distributions. Because we are unable to arrive at a simple characterization of the empirical distributions  $\hat{\mu} = \frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\delta_{\widehat{\theta}_j}$  and  $\hat{\mu}' := \frac{1}{p}\sum_{j=1}^{p}\delta_{\widehat{t}_j}$  in the correlated design case, we were unable to follow a strategy similar to [56].

Instead, we resort to a smoothing argument. For penalized regression estimators with differentiable penalties, the subgradient  $\widehat{\pmb{t}}$  is a function of the estimate  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}^f$ . Thus, for smooth procedures, the expression corresponding to Eq. (35) is a deterministic function only of the estimate. Thus, the replacement of the quantities in (35) by their fixed-design counterparts can be justified via analysis of the distribution of the estimate  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}$  individually. The Lasso penalty  $\| \pmb {\theta}\| _1$  is not smooth, so that  $\widehat{\pmb{t}}$  is not a deterministic function of  $\widehat{\pmb{\theta}}$ . To handle this, we introduce the  $\alpha$ -smoothed Lasso, in which we replace the  $\ell_1$ -penalty by a smooth approximation in the original Lasso objective (1). We prove a characterization of the  $\alpha$ -smoothed Lasso analogous to Theorem 5, and use this to establish the success of the debiasing procedure corresponding to the smoothed estimator. Finally, we argue that the debiased Lasso estimate is well-approximated by the debiased  $\alpha$ -smoothed Lasso estimate for small enough smoothing parameter, and show that Theorem 10 follows. The details of this argument are provided in Appendix B.7.

# Supplement to 'The Lasso with general Gaussian designs with applications to hypothesis testing.'

# A. Preliminaries.

A.1. A Gaussian width tradeoff. Define the descent cone

$$
\mathcal {D} (\boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) := \left\{\boldsymbol {v} \in L ^ {2} \Big | \mathbb {E} \big [ F (\boldsymbol {v}; \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) \big ] \leq 0 \right\}.
$$

The Gaussian width in Eq. (13) can be seen as the maximal value of the correlation  $\langle \pmb{v},\pmb{g}\rangle_{L^2} / \| \pmb{v}\|_{L^2}\| \pmb{g}\|_{L^2}$  subject to the constraint  $\pmb{v}\in \mathcal{D}(\pmb {x},\pmb {\Sigma})$ . In this section, we quantify the sensitivity of this maximal correlation to small relaxations of this constraint. This is a central tool in establishing bounds on the solutions to Eqs. (9a) and (9b) (see the proof of Theorem 5(a) in Section A.4).

LEMMA A.1. Fix  $\pmb{\Sigma} \in \mathbb{S}_{\geq 0}^{p}$  with singular values bounded  $0 < \kappa_{\min} \leq \kappa_{j}(\pmb{\Sigma}) \leq \kappa_{\max} < \infty$  for all  $j$ . Define  $\kappa_{\mathrm{cond}} := \kappa_{\mathrm{max}} / \kappa_{\mathrm{min}}$ . Let  $\pmb{x} \in \{-1,0,1\}^{p}$  with  $\| \pmb{x} \|_0 / p \geq \nu_{\min} > 0$ . Let  $S = \operatorname{supp}(\pmb{x})$ . Then, for any  $\pmb{v} \in L^2$  and any  $\epsilon > 0$ , we have either

$$
\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \langle \boldsymbol {v}, \boldsymbol {g} \rangle_ {L ^ {2}} \leq \left(\mathcal {G} (\boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) + \epsilon\right) \| \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {L ^ {2}}, \tag {36}
$$

or

$$
\frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \mathbb {E} \left[ F (\boldsymbol {v}; \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) \right] \geq \frac {\nu_ {\min } ^ {1 / 2} \epsilon}{\kappa_ {\max } ^ {1 / 2} (2 + \kappa_ {\operatorname {c o n d}})} \| \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {L ^ {2}}. \tag {37}
$$

PROOF OF LEMMA A.1. Under the change-of-variables  $\pmb{w} = \sqrt{p}\pmb{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\pmb{v}$ , we may alternatively write (13) as

$$
\mathcal{G}(\boldsymbol {x},\boldsymbol {\Sigma}) = \sup_{\substack{\boldsymbol {w}\in \mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol {x},\boldsymbol{I}_{p})\\ \| \boldsymbol{\Sigma}^{1 / 2}\boldsymbol {w}\|_{L^{2}}^{2}\leq p}}\frac{1}{p}\mathbb{E}\Big[\boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{g}}\Big],
$$

where  $\tilde{\pmb{g}} = \pmb{\Sigma}^{1/2}\pmb{g}$  and  $\pmb{g}$  is interpreted as the identity function in  $L^2$ . The Lagrangian for this problem reads:

$$
\mathcal {L} (\pmb {w}; \kappa , \xi) := \frac {1}{p} \mathbb {E} \left[ \pmb {w} ^ {\top} \tilde {\pmb {g}} \right] + \frac {\kappa}{2} \left(1 - \frac {1}{p} \mathbb {E} \left[ \| \pmb {\Sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \pmb {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right]\right) - \frac {\xi}{p} \mathbb {E} \left[ \sum_ {j \in S} x _ {j} w _ {j} + \| \pmb {w} _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1} \right],
$$

where the Langrange multipliers  $\kappa, \xi$  are restricted to be non-negative. First, we bound the dual optimal Lagrange multipliers. We bound

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {\kappa}{2} + \frac {1}{p} \mathbb {E} \left[ \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\top} \tilde {\boldsymbol {g}} - \frac {\kappa \kappa_ {\min }}{2} \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \xi \left(\sum_ {j \in S} x _ {j} w _ {j} + \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1}\right) \right] \geq \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {w}; \kappa , \xi) \\ \geq \frac {\kappa}{2} + \frac {1}{p} \mathbb {E} \left[ \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\top} \tilde {\boldsymbol {g}} - \frac {\kappa \kappa_ {\max }}{2} \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \xi \left(\sum_ {j \in S} x _ {j} w _ {j} + \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1}\right) \right]. \tag {38} \\ \end{array}
$$

The expected value appearing in the upper bound is maximized by maximizing the integrand for each value of  $\tilde{g}$ . Because the integrand is separable across coordinates, we may do this explicitly. The maximal value of the integrand at fixed  $\tilde{g}$  is

$$
\frac {\kappa}{2} + \frac {1}{2 p \kappa \kappa_ {\mathrm {m i n}}} \sum_ {j \in S} (\tilde {g} _ {j} - \xi x _ {j}) ^ {2} + \frac {1}{p \kappa \kappa_ {\mathrm {m i n}}} \sum_ {j \in S ^ {c}} \left(\frac {\tilde {g} _ {j} ^ {2}}{2} - \xi \mathsf {M} _ {\xi} (\tilde {g} _ {j})\right),
$$

where  $\mathsf{M}_{\xi}(\tilde{g}_j)$  is the Moreau envelope of the  $\ell_1$  -norm

$$
\mathsf {M} _ {\xi} (y) := \inf  _ {x \in \mathbb {R}} \left\{\frac {1}{2 \xi} (y - x) ^ {2} + | x | \right\}.
$$

Because  $\xi \mathsf{M}_{\xi}(\tilde{g}_j) \geq 0$ , we have  $\mathbb{E}[\tilde{g}_j^2 / 2 - \xi \mathsf{M}_{\xi}(\tilde{g}_j)] \leq \mathbb{E}[\tilde{g}_j^2 / 2] \leq \mathbb{E}[(\tilde{g}_j - \xi x)^2 / 2] \leq (\kappa_{\max} + \xi^2) / 2$  whenever  $x = \pm 1$ . Thus,

$$
\frac {\kappa}{2} + \frac {1}{\kappa} \frac {\kappa_ {\text {c o n d}} + \xi^ {2} / \kappa_ {\min }}{2} \geq \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {w} \in L ^ {2}} \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {w}; \kappa , \xi).
$$

This further implies that

$$
\inf  _ {\kappa , \xi \geq 0} \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {w} \in L ^ {2}} \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {w}; \kappa , \xi) \leq \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {w} \in L ^ {2}} \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {w}; 1, \kappa_ {\min } ^ {1 / 2}) = 1 + \frac {\kappa_ {\text {c o n d}}}{2}. \tag {39}
$$

Similarly, maximizing the right-hand side of Eq. (38) explicitly and using  $\tilde{g}_j^2 / 2 - \xi \mathsf{M}_{\xi}(\tilde{g}_j) \geq 0$ ,

$$
\sup  _ {\boldsymbol {w} \in L ^ {2}} \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {w}; \kappa , \xi) \geq \frac {\kappa}{2} + \frac {1}{\kappa} \frac {| S | \left(1 / \kappa_ {\text {c o n d}} + \xi^ {2} / \kappa_ {\max }\right)}{2 p}. \tag {40}
$$

If either  $\kappa /2 > 1 + \kappa_{\mathrm{cond}} / 2$  or  $\xi^2 /\kappa_{\mathrm{max}} > 4(1 + \kappa_{\mathrm{cond}} / 2)^2 /(|S| / p)$ , then  $\sup_{\boldsymbol {w}\in L^2}\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol {w};\kappa ,\xi) > 1 + \kappa_{\mathrm{cond}} / 2$ . Combining the previous two displays, we conclude that  $\inf_{\kappa ,\xi \geq 0}\sup_{\boldsymbol {w}\in L^2}\mathcal{L}(\boldsymbol {w};\kappa ,\xi)$  is achieved at some

$$
\kappa^ {*} \leq 2 + \kappa_ {\text {c o n d}} \quad \text {a n d} \quad \xi^ {*} \leq \frac {\kappa_ {\max } ^ {1 / 2} (2 + \kappa_ {\text {c o n d}})}{(| S | / p) ^ {1 / 2}}. \tag {41}
$$

Since the constraints on  $\pmb{w}$  are strictly feasible, strong duality holds:

$$
\sup  _ {\boldsymbol {w} \in L ^ {2}} \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {w}; \kappa^ {*}, \xi^ {*}) = \mathcal {G} (\boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}).
$$

The dual optimal variable  $\xi^{*}$  quantifies the tradeoff we seek to control, as we now show. For any function  $\boldsymbol{w}:\mathbb{R}^p\to \mathbb{R}^p$ , let  $\bar{\boldsymbol{w}}:\mathbb{R}^p\to \mathbb{R}^p$  be defined by  $\bar{\boldsymbol{w}} (\boldsymbol {g}) = \sqrt{p}\boldsymbol {w}(\boldsymbol {g}) / \mathbb{E}[\| \boldsymbol {w}(\boldsymbol {g})\|_{\Sigma}^2 ]^{1 / 2}$  where  $\| \boldsymbol {w}\|_{\Sigma}^{2} = \boldsymbol{w}^{\top}\boldsymbol {\Sigma}\boldsymbol{w}$ . Then

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{p} \langle \bar {\boldsymbol {w}}, \tilde {\boldsymbol {g}} \rangle_ {L ^ {2}} - \frac {\xi^ {*}}{p} \mathbb {E} \left[ \sum_ {j \in S} x _ {j} \bar {w} _ {j} (\boldsymbol {g}) + \| \bar {\boldsymbol {w}} _ {S ^ {c}} (\boldsymbol {g}) \| _ {1} \right] \\ = \frac {1}{p} \mathbb {E} [ \bar {\boldsymbol {w}} (\boldsymbol {g}) ^ {\top} \tilde {\boldsymbol {g}} ] + \frac {\kappa^ {*}}{2} \left(1 - \frac {1}{p} \mathbb {E} [ \| \bar {\boldsymbol {w}} \| _ {\Sigma} ^ {2} ]\right) - \frac {\xi^ {*}}{p} \mathbb {E} \left[ \sum_ {j \in S} x _ {j} \bar {w} _ {j} (\boldsymbol {g}) + \| \bar {\boldsymbol {w}} _ {S ^ {c}} (\boldsymbol {g}) \| _ {1} \right] \\ \leq \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {w} \in L ^ {2}} \mathcal {L} (\boldsymbol {w}; \kappa^ {*}, \xi^ {*}) = \mathcal {G} (\boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}), \\ \end{array}
$$

where in the first equality we used that  $\mathbb{E}[\| \bar{\boldsymbol{w}} (\boldsymbol {g})\|_{\Sigma}^2 ] / p = 1$  . We conclude that for any  $\epsilon >0$

$$
\text {e i t h e r} \quad \frac {1}{p} \langle \bar {\boldsymbol {w}}, \tilde {\boldsymbol {g}} \rangle_ {L ^ {2}} \leq \mathcal {G} (\boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) + \epsilon \quad \text {o r} \quad \frac {1}{p} \mathbb {E} \left[ \sum_ {j \in S} x _ {j} \bar {w} _ {j} (\tilde {\boldsymbol {g}}) + \| \bar {\boldsymbol {w}} (\boldsymbol {g}) _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1} \right] \geq \frac {\epsilon}{\xi^ {*}}.
$$

Plugging in  $\boldsymbol{w} = \mathbb{E}[\| \boldsymbol{w}(\boldsymbol{g})\|_{\Sigma}^2]^{1/2}\bar{\boldsymbol{w}} / \sqrt{p}$  and the upper bound on  $\xi^*$  in (41), the lemma follows.

# A.2. Some properties of the Gaussian width.

PROOF OF LEMMA 1. Notice that

$$
(42) \sup  _ {\substack {\boldsymbol {v} \in \mathcal {D} (\boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) \\ \| \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} \leq 1}} \frac {1}{\sqrt {p}} \langle \boldsymbol {v}, \boldsymbol {g} \rangle_ {L ^ {2}} = \sup  _ {\substack {\boldsymbol {w} \in \mathcal {D} (\boldsymbol {x}, \mathbf {I} _ {p}) \\ \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} \leq 1}} \frac {\| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {L ^ {2}} \langle \boldsymbol {\Sigma} \boldsymbol {w} , \boldsymbol {g} \rangle_ {L ^ {2}}}{\sqrt {p} \| \boldsymbol {\Sigma} \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {L ^ {2}}} \leq \frac {\kappa_ {\max} ^ {1 / 2}}{\kappa_ {\min} ^ {1 / 2}} \sup  _ {\substack {\boldsymbol {w} \in \mathcal {D} (\boldsymbol {x}, \mathbf {I} _ {p}) \\ \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {L ^ {2}} ^ {2} \leq 1}} \frac {\langle \boldsymbol {\Sigma} \boldsymbol {w} , \boldsymbol {g} \rangle_ {L ^ {2}}}{\sqrt {p} \kappa_ {\max} ^ {1 / 2}},
$$

where in the equality we have used that  $\boldsymbol{w} \leftrightarrow \| \boldsymbol{w} \|_{L^2} \boldsymbol{\Sigma} \boldsymbol{w} / \| \boldsymbol{\Sigma} \boldsymbol{w} \|_{L^2}$  is a bijection between the sets over which the suprema are taken, and in the inequality we have used that the supremum is positive (because  $\boldsymbol{w} = \mathbf{0}$  is feasible) and  $\| \boldsymbol{w} \|_{L^2} / \| \boldsymbol{\Sigma} \boldsymbol{w} \|_{L^2} \leq \kappa_{\min}^{-1/2}$ . The Lagrangian for the maximization on the right-hand side is

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathcal {L} _ {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}} (\boldsymbol {w}; \kappa , \xi) := \frac {1}{\sqrt {p} \kappa_ {\max } ^ {1 / 2}} \mathbb {E} \left[ \boldsymbol {w} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {g} \right] + \frac {\kappa}{2} \left(1 - \mathbb {E} \left[ \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right]\right) - \frac {\xi}{\sqrt {p}} \mathbb {E} \left[ \sum_ {j \in S} x _ {j} w _ {j} + \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1} \right] \\ = \frac {\kappa}{2} + \mathbb {E} \left[ \frac {\pmb {w} ^ {\top} \pmb {\Sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \pmb {g}}{\sqrt {p} \kappa_ {\max} ^ {1 / 2}} - \frac {\kappa}{2} \| \pmb {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \frac {\xi}{\sqrt {p}} \left(\sum_ {j \in S} x _ {j} w _ {j} + \| \pmb {w} _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1}\right) \right]. \\ \end{array}
$$

The optimal  $\pmb{w} \in L^{2}$  maximizes the integrand for almost every  $\pmb{g}$ , whence

$$
\sup  _ {\boldsymbol {w} \in L ^ {2}} \mathcal {L} _ {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}} (\boldsymbol {w}; \kappa , \xi) = \frac {\kappa}{2} + \mathbb {E} \left[ \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {w} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}} \left\{\frac {\boldsymbol {w} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {g}}{\sqrt {p} \kappa_ {\max } ^ {1 / 2}} - \frac {\kappa}{2} \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \frac {\xi}{\sqrt {p}} \left(\sum_ {j \in S} x _ {j} w _ {j} + \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1}\right) \right\} \right].
$$

We emphasize that the dummy variable  $\pmb{w}$  is in  $L^2$  on the left-hand side and  $\mathbb{R}^p$  on the right-hand side. We apply the Sudakov-Fernique inequality [1, Theorem 2.2.3] to upper bound the expectation in the preceding display. Indeed, for  $\pmb{w},\pmb{w}'\in \mathbb{R}^p$ , we have  $\mathbb{E}[\pmb{w}^\top \pmb{\Sigma}^{1 / 2}\pmb {g} / \kappa_{\max}^{1 / 2}] = \mathbb{E}[\pmb{w}^\top \pmb {g}] = 0$  and  $\mathrm{Var}((\pmb {w} - \pmb {w}')^\top \pmb {\Sigma}^{1 / 2}\pmb {g} / \kappa_{\max}^{1 / 2})\leq \mathrm{Var}((\pmb {w} - \pmb {w}')^\top \pmb {g})$  because  $\| \pmb{\Sigma}^{1 / 2} / \kappa_{\max}^{1 / 2}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\leq 1$ . Thus, the Sudakov-Fernique inequality implies

$$
\begin{array}{l} \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {w} \in L ^ {2}} \mathcal {L} _ {\boldsymbol {\Sigma}} (\boldsymbol {w}; \kappa , \xi) \leq \frac {\kappa}{2} + \mathbb {E} \left[ \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {w} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}} \left\{\frac {\boldsymbol {w} ^ {\top} \boldsymbol {g}}{\sqrt {p}} - \frac {\kappa}{2} \| \boldsymbol {w} \| _ {2} ^ {2} - \frac {\xi}{\sqrt {p}} \left(\sum_ {j \in S} x _ {j} w _ {j} + \| \boldsymbol {w} _ {S ^ {c}} \| _ {1}\right) \right\} \right] \\ = \sup  _ {\boldsymbol {w} \in L ^ {2}} \mathcal {L} _ {\mathbf {I} _ {p}} (\boldsymbol {w}; \kappa , \xi). \\ \end{array}
$$

For any  $\kappa, \xi \geq 0$ ,  $\sup_{\boldsymbol{w} \in L^2} \mathcal{L}_{\boldsymbol{\Sigma}}(\boldsymbol{w}; \kappa, \xi) \geq \sup_{\substack{\boldsymbol{w} \in \mathcal{D}(\boldsymbol{x}, \mathbf{l}_p) \\ \| \boldsymbol{w} \|_{L^2}^2 \leq 1}} \frac{\langle \boldsymbol{\Sigma} \boldsymbol{w}, \boldsymbol{g} \rangle_{L^2}}{\sqrt{p} \kappa_{\max}^{1/2}}$ , whence, by Eq. (42),

$$
\mathcal{G}(\boldsymbol {x},\boldsymbol {\Sigma})\leq \kappa_{\text{cond}}^{1 / 2}\sup_{\boldsymbol {w}\in L^{2}}\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{I}_{p}}(\boldsymbol {w};\kappa ,\xi).
$$

Note that  $\mathcal{L}_{\mathbf{I}_p}(\boldsymbol {w};\kappa ,\xi)$  is the Lagrangian for the optimization Eq. (13) defining  $\mathcal{G}(\boldsymbol {x},\mathbf{I}_p)$ . Because the constraints on  $\boldsymbol{w}$  in this optimization are strictly feasible, strong duality holds. Thus, Eq. (14) follows by taking the infimum over  $\kappa ,\xi \geq 0$  in the preceding display.

PROOF OF PROPOSITION 2. By the Lagrangian calculations in the proof of Lemma A.1,

$$
\mathcal {G} (\boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) := \frac {1}{p} \langle \boldsymbol {v} ^ {*}, \boldsymbol {g} \rangle_ {L ^ {2}},
$$

where

$$
\boldsymbol {v} ^ {*} (\boldsymbol {g}) = \arg \min  _ {\boldsymbol {v} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}} \left\{\frac {\kappa^ {*}}{2} \| (\kappa^ {*}) ^ {- 1} \boldsymbol {g} - \boldsymbol {v} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \xi^ {*} F (\boldsymbol {v}; \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) \right\},
$$

and  $\pmb{g}$  is the identity function of  $L_{2}$ . We have

$$
\kappa^ {*} \geq \frac {(| S | / p) / (2 \kappa_ {\mathrm {c o n d}})}{1 + \kappa_ {\mathrm {c o n d}} / 2} \geq \frac {c ^ {\prime} | S |}{p},
$$

where we have used (39) and (40) in the first inequality. The constant  $c' > 0$  depends only on  $\kappa_{\mathrm{cond}}$ , and may change values at each appearance. Because proximal operators are 1-Lipschitz [62], we see that  $v^{*}$  is  $c'p / |S|$ -Lipschitz in  $g$ .

Let  $\pmb{w}^{*} \coloneqq \pmb{\Sigma}^{-1/2}\pmb{v}^{*}$ , and define  $\pmb{v}^{(1)} \coloneqq \pmb{\Sigma}^{1/2}\pmb{w}^{(1)}$ , where

$$
\boldsymbol {w} _ {S} ^ {(1)} := \boldsymbol {w} _ {S} ^ {*} - \frac {F (\boldsymbol {v} ^ {*} ; \boldsymbol {x} , \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) _ {+}}{| S |} \boldsymbol {x} _ {S}, \qquad \boldsymbol {w} _ {S ^ {c}} ^ {(1)} := \boldsymbol {w} _ {S ^ {c}} ^ {*},
$$

and  $F(\pmb{v}^{*}; \pmb{x}, \pmb{\Sigma})_{+} = \max \{0, F(\pmb{v}^{*}; \pmb{x}, \pmb{\Sigma})\}$  denotes the positive part of  $F(\pmb{v}^{*}; \pmb{x}, \pmb{\Sigma})_{+}$ . Note that

$$
F (\boldsymbol {v} ^ {(1)}; \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) = F (\boldsymbol {v} ^ {*}; \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) - F (\boldsymbol {v} ^ {*}; \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) _ {+} = F (\boldsymbol {v} ^ {*}; \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) _ {-} \leq 0,
$$

$$
\frac {\| \pmb {v} ^ {(1)} - \pmb {v} ^ {*} \| _ {2}}{\sqrt {p}} \leq \frac {\kappa_ {\max} ^ {1 / 2} \| \pmb {w} ^ {(1)} - \pmb {w} ^ {*} \| _ {2}}{\sqrt {p}} = \frac {\kappa_ {\max} ^ {1 / 2} F (\pmb {v} ^ {*} ; \pmb {x} , \pmb {\Sigma}) _ {+}}{\sqrt {p} | S | ^ {1 / 2}}.
$$

Define  $\pmb{v}^{(2)} = \sqrt{p}\pmb{v}^{(1)} / \| \pmb{v}^{(1)}\|_{2}$ . Note

$$
\frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {v} ^ {(2)} \right\| _ {2}}{\sqrt {p}} = 1, \quad F (\boldsymbol {v} ^ {(2)}; \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) = \frac {\sqrt {p}}{\left\| \boldsymbol {v} ^ {(1)} \right\| _ {2}} F (\boldsymbol {v} ^ {(1)}; \boldsymbol {x}, \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) \leq 0,
$$

$$
\frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {v} ^ {(2)} - \boldsymbol {v} ^ {(1)} \right\| _ {2}}{\sqrt {p}} = \left| 1 - \frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {v} ^ {(1)} \right\| _ {2}}{\sqrt {p}} \right| \leq \left| 1 - \frac {\left\| \boldsymbol {v} ^ {*} \right\| _ {2}}{\sqrt {p}} \right| + \frac {\kappa_ {\max } ^ {1 / 2} F (\boldsymbol {v} ^ {*} ; \boldsymbol {x} , \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) _ {+}}{\sqrt {p} | S | ^ {1 / 2}}.
$$

The first line of the previous display implies that  $\pmb{v}^{(2)}$  satisfies the constraints in Eq. (12). Moreover, using Cauchy-Schwartz

$$
\begin{array}{l} \frac {1}{p} \left| \langle \boldsymbol {v} ^ {(1)}, \boldsymbol {g} \rangle_ {L ^ {2}} - \langle \boldsymbol {v} ^ {*}, \boldsymbol {g} \rangle_ {L ^ {2}} \right| \leq \frac {\mathbb {E} \left[ \| \boldsymbol {v} ^ {(1)} - \boldsymbol {v} ^ {*} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right] ^ {1 / 2}}{\sqrt {p}} \\ \leq \mathbb {E} \left[ \left(\left| 1 - \frac {\| \boldsymbol {v} ^ {*} \| _ {2}}{\sqrt {p}} \right| + \frac {2 \kappa_ {\operatorname* {m a x}} ^ {1 / 2} F (\boldsymbol {v} ^ {*} ; \boldsymbol {x} , \boldsymbol {\Sigma}) _ {+}}{\sqrt {p} | S | ^ {1 / 2}}\right) ^ {2} \right] ^ {1 / 2} \\ \end{array}
$$

Because  $\mathbb{E}[\| \pmb{v}^* \|_2^2] / p = 1$ ,  $\mathbb{E}[F(\pmb{v}^*; \pmb{x}, \pmb{\Sigma})] \leq 0$ , the quantity  $\| \pmb{v}^* \|_2 / \sqrt{p}$  is  $1 / \sqrt{p}$ -Lipschitz in  $\pmb{v}^*$ , the quantity  $F(\pmb{v}^*; \pmb{x}, \pmb{\Sigma})_+ / (\sqrt{p} |S|^{1/2})$  is  $\kappa_{\min}^{-1/2} / \sqrt{p}$ -Lipschitz in  $\pmb{v}^*$ , and  $\pmb{v}^*$  is  $c' p / |S|$ -Lipschitz in  $\pmb{g}$ , we have by Gaussian concentration of Lipschitz functions that the right-hand side is bound above by  $c' \sqrt{p} / |S|$  for some  $c'$  depending on  $\kappa_{\mathrm{cond}}$ . Because  $\langle \pmb{v}^*, \pmb{g} \rangle_{L^2} = \mathcal{G}(\pmb{x}, \pmb{\Sigma})$ , we conclude that  $\mathcal{G}_d(\pmb{x}, \pmb{\Sigma}) \geq \mathcal{G}(\pmb{x}, \pmb{\Sigma}) - c' \sqrt{p} / s$ . The second bound comes from the fact that  $\mathcal{G}(\pmb{x}, \pmb{\Sigma}) \leq c' \sqrt{s \log(p / s) / p}$  by Lemma 1 and the fact that  $\omega^*(\varepsilon) \sim (1 + o(\varepsilon)) \sqrt{2\varepsilon \log(1 / \varepsilon)}$ , and the  $\mathcal{G}_d(\pmb{x}, \pmb{\Sigma}) \geq 0$ .

A.3. The  $\alpha$ -smoothed Lasso. Controlling the debiased Lasso (Theorem 10) will require a smoothing argument in which we replace the  $\ell_1$ -penalty by a differentiable approximation. We call the estimator using the differentiable approximation the  $\alpha$ -smoothed Lasso. Throughout the appendix, all theory is developed for non-smoothed and smoothed Lasso in a unified way. Results about the Lasso estimate and residual will be instances of these general results.

For  $\alpha > 0$ , define the Moreau envelope of the  $\ell_1$ -norm

$$
\mathrm {M} _ {\alpha} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) := \inf  _ {\boldsymbol {b} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}} \left\{\frac {1}{2 \alpha} \| \boldsymbol {\theta} - \boldsymbol {b} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \| \boldsymbol {b} \| _ {1} \right\}, \tag {43}
$$

and define  $\mathsf{M}_0(\pmb {\theta}) = \| \pmb {\theta}\| _1$ . Notice that this coincides with the H $\text{uber}$  loss. In particular, for all  $\pmb {\theta}\in \mathbb{R}^p$

$$
\left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {1} - \frac {p \alpha}{2} \leq \mathrm {M} _ {\alpha} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \leq \left\| \boldsymbol {\theta} \right\| _ {1}. \tag {44}
$$

For all  $\alpha \geq 0$ , define the  $\alpha$ -smoothed Lasso in the random-design model

$$
\begin{array}{l} \widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\alpha} := \arg \min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}} \left\{\frac {1}{2 n} \| \boldsymbol {y} - \boldsymbol {X} \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \frac {\lambda}{\sqrt {n}} \left(\mathrm {M} _ {\alpha} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) - \| \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \| _ {1}\right) \right\} \tag {45} \\ =: \arg \min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}} \mathcal {R} _ {\alpha} (\boldsymbol {\theta}), \\ \end{array}
$$

where the term  $-\|\pmb{\theta}^{*}\|_{1}$  is added to the definition of  $\mathcal{R}_{\alpha}(\pmb{\theta})$  for future convenience. Define the  $\alpha$ -smoothed Lasso in the fixed-design model

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {\theta}} _ {\alpha} ^ {f} := \eta_ {\alpha} \left(\boldsymbol {y} ^ {f}, \zeta\right) := \arg \min  _ {\boldsymbol {\theta} \in \mathbb {R} ^ {p}} \left\{\frac {\zeta}{2} \| \boldsymbol {y} ^ {f} - \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} \| _ {2} ^ {2} + \frac {\lambda}{\sqrt {n}} \mathrm {M} _ {\alpha} (\boldsymbol {\theta}) \right\}, \tag {46}
$$

$$
\widehat {\boldsymbol {y}} _ {\alpha} (\boldsymbol {y} ^ {f}, \zeta) := \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \eta_ {\alpha} (\boldsymbol {y} ^ {f}, \zeta).
$$

Denote the in-sample prediction risk and degrees-of-freedom of the  $\alpha$ -smoothed Lasso in the fixed-design model by

$$
\mathsf {R} _ {\alpha} (\tau^ {2}, \zeta) := \mathbb {E} \left[ \| \widehat {\boldsymbol {y}} _ {\alpha} (\boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} + \tau \boldsymbol {g} / \sqrt {n}, \zeta) - \boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} \| _ {2} ^ {2} \right],
$$

$$
\begin{array}{l} \mathrm {d f} _ {\alpha} \left(\tau^ {2}, \zeta\right) := \frac {\sqrt {n}}{\tau} \mathbb {E} \left[ \left\langle \widehat {\boldsymbol {y}} _ {\alpha} \left(\boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} + \tau \boldsymbol {g} / \sqrt {n}, \zeta\right), \boldsymbol {g} \right\rangle \right] \tag {47} \\ = \mathbb {E} \left[ \operatorname {d i v} \widehat {\boldsymbol {y}} _ {\alpha} \left(\boldsymbol {\Sigma} ^ {1 / 2} \boldsymbol {\theta} ^ {*} + \tau \boldsymbol {g} / \sqrt {n}\right) \right], \\ \end{array}
$$

where the expectation is over  $\pmb{g} \sim \mathsf{N}(\mathbf{0}_p, \mathbf{I}_p)$ . Let  $\tau_{\alpha}^{*}, \zeta_{\alpha}^{*}$  be solutions to the system of equations

$$
\tau_ {\alpha} ^ {2} = \sigma^ {2} + \mathrm {R} _ {\alpha} \left(\tau_ {\alpha} ^ {2}, \zeta_ {\alpha}\right), \tag {48a}
$$

$$
\zeta_ {\alpha} = 1 - \frac {\mathrm {d f} _ {\alpha} \left(\tau_ {\alpha} ^ {2} , \zeta_ {\alpha}\right)}{n}. \tag {48b}
$$

We refer to these equations as the  $\alpha$ -smoothed fixed point equations. For  $\alpha = 0$ , these definitions agree with the corresponding definitions for the Lasso. The solutions  $\tau_{\alpha}^{*}$ ,  $\zeta_{\alpha}^{*}$  are well-defined, and can be bounded in terms of model parameters.

LEMMA A.2. The fixed-point equations (48a) and (48b) satisfy the following properties.
